交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31号

10月金融数据点评

宏观 
报告日期 2026—11-16 专题报告 
   10月金融数据呈现"社融微升、信贷负增、M1 修复、M2续
降"的组合。社融存量同比增速较 9 月回升 0.1 个百分点
至7.2%，主要得益于政府债券集中发行；单月新增社融约
1.15 万亿元，同比多增逾 3000 亿元，但增量几乎全部由
政府部门贡献。金融机构新增人民币贷款-1000 亿元，同
比少增约 3200 亿元，票据融资退潮是主要拖累，企业中
长期贷款边际改善但居民端连续净偿还。货币端 M1 同比
回升至 4.4%、M2 同比降至 7.1%，剪刀差收敛至 2.7 个百
分点，资金活化出现阶段性修复信号，但其中包含低基数
因素，尚不能确认企业内生扩张重启。综合来看，10月数
据延续了"降速提质"的存量验证逻辑：财政托底使总量读
数企稳，但私人部门融资需求仍偏弱，政策重心正由"宽
松力度"转向"传导效率"。

国贸期货  樊梦真 
从业资格证号： F3035483 
投资咨询证号： Z0014706

### Page 2

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31号

➢ 事件： 
2026 年 11 月 13 日，中国人民银行发布《2026 年 10 月金融统计数据报告》。10 月末社会融资规模存
量 469.16 万亿元，同比增长 7.2%（前值为 7.1%）；前十个月社融增量累计 28.61 万亿元，比上年同期少
2.29 万亿元，10 月单月新增约 1.15 万亿元，同比多增逾 3000 亿元。信贷方面，10 月末人民币贷款余额
283.33万亿元，同比增长4.7%（前值4.8%）；前十个月人民币贷款增加11.42万亿元，10月单月少增1000
亿元，同比少增约3200亿元。10 月末 M2余额359.06万亿元，同比增长7.1%（前值7.2%）； M1余额116.9
万亿元，同比增长 4.4%（前值3.2%）。 
图表 1：社融和信贷  图表 2：M1和 M2

资料来源：Wind、国贸期货研究院  资料来源：Wind、国贸期货研究院

➢ 点评： 
 总量：社融增速止跌微升，但改善几乎全部来自政府债单极支撑 
10 月社融存量同比 7.2%，结束此前连续两个月的回落，单月新增社融约 1.15 万亿元，同比多增逾
3000亿元。从结构归因看，改善动力高度集中于政府债券：10月政府债净融资约1.18-1.19万亿元，占当
月社融增量比重超过100%，剔除政府债后的社融增速降至约5.2%，私人部门融资需求仍在走弱。前十个月
直接融资合计占社融增量约 53%，较前三季度进一步提升，超过贷款占比约 14 个百分点，融资结构由信贷
主导转向"政府债+直接融资"双支柱的格局进一步固化。从资金价格信号看，10 月企业新发放贷款加权平
均利率约2.9%，同比低约0.2个百分点；个人住房贷款利率约3.1%，与上年同期基本持平，金融供给端条
件仍处历史宽松区间。因此，总量读数的企稳更多反映财政发力和直接融资渠道畅通，而非实体自发融资

需求的反转。

 结构：政府债独撑，企业债修复、非标分化 
10 月社融分项呈"政府债独撑、信贷拖累、直接融资补位"格局。政府债券净融资约 1.18 万亿元、同
比多增约 7000 亿元，是社融同比多增的唯一正向来源；社融口径人民币贷款减少约 5800 亿元、同比多减
约 5700 亿元，是最主要拖累。直接融资方面，企业债券净融资约 3400 亿元、同比多增约 950 亿元，在债
券利率低位环境下，债券对信贷的替代效应继续显现；股票融资约 350 亿元、同比略少增。表外融资边际
改善：委托贷款增加约1860亿元、同比多增约200亿元，与政策性金融工具通过委托贷款通道投放有关；
未贴现银行承兑汇票减少约 1700 亿元但同比少减约 1200 亿元；信托贷款减少约 415 亿元。综合看，10 月
社融的结构特征可概括为"财政补位、票据退潮"——政府信用扩张对冲了银行表内信贷的季节性收缩，但
直接融资对信贷的替代并非线性推进，单月节奏仍存在波动。

 信贷：票据退潮拖累单月负增，企业中长期边际修复、居民持续收缩 
10 月新增人民币贷款-1000 亿元，为继 4 月、7 月之后年内第三次单月负增，同比少增约 3200 亿元。
结构拆解显示，票据融资同比少增约8000亿元，是信贷负增的主因——前期集中投放的票据陆续到期，叠
加监管对票据冲量约束强化，银行"以票充贷"空间明显压缩。剔除票据后，企业贷款实际新增约 1500 亿
元、同比多增约3000亿元，其中企业中长期贷款新增约1600亿元、同比多增约1300亿元，可能初步体现
新型政策性金融工具与专项债配套的融资支持，但改善幅度有限、持续性仍待观察。 
居民端则延续净偿还：10 月住户贷款减少约 4300 亿元，同比多减约 700 亿元；短期贷款减少约 2700
亿元，中长期贷款减少约 1560 亿元、同比多减约 900 亿元。前三季度累计住户贷款已减少 1.03 万亿元，
10 月单月再减约 4300 亿元，前 10 月累计减少 1.46万亿元，而去年同期为净增加 1.10万亿元，居民部门
去杠杆已从慢变量演化为持续性硬读数，与 10月 30城商品房成交面积同比降幅扩大至 18%相互印证。

 货币供应：M1 回升至4.4%、M2 降至 7.1% 
10 月末 M1 同比 4.4%，较 9 月回升1.2 个百分点；M2同比7.1%，较 9月再降 0.3个百分点；M2-M1 剪
刀差由 4.0 个百分点收窄至 2.7 个百分点，为年内较优水平。M1 的回升包含两方面因素：一方面，去年同
期基数偏低对读数形成支撑，不能完全归因为企业经营活跃度实质改善；另一方面，财政资金拨付提速、
企业活期存款边际回流、以及政策性金融工具落地带来的项目资金活化，确实对 M1 形成正向拉动。M2 继
续回落则与信贷派生偏弱、非银存款波动、财政存款淤积有关——10月财政性存款前十个月累计增加3.03
万亿元，政府债募集资金属阶段性滞留国库，尚未完全转化为一般性存款。 
存款结构显示，住户存款前十个月增加 8.47 万亿元，居民端储蓄倾向仍强；企业存款增加 1.91 万亿
元，活化程度边际改善但仍偏谨慎。综合判断，M1 的修复是积极信号，但需结合后续订单、PPI 与项目开

分析师介绍 
樊梦真：国贸期货宏观金融首席分析师，毕业于中国科学院数学与系统科学研究院。国债、外汇研究员，
专注于宏观金融领域的研究，善于利用跨专业背景对宏观经济、外汇、利率及其衍生品进行分析，熟练运
用数理工具为客户提供专业服务与交易策略。曾获期货日报“最佳宏观金融期货分析师”称号。

公司簡介 
志聖成立於 1966 年，目前資本額 16.2 億
新台幣，為 G2C 集團當中的核心成員之一，
以紫外光、烘烤、壓膜塗佈、電漿製程等技
術提供相關壓膜、貼、撕膜、烘烤、電鍍相
關設備。公司初期以 PCB 設備起家，而後
伴隨台灣電子產業升級，切入半導體、組裝、
面板、太陽能等產業，近年陸續在 IC 載板、
高階 HDI、先進封裝、HBM 等高附加價值
市場發光發熱。1-3Q26 營收以產業別劃
分，半導體佔約 45%、先進 PCB 28%、傳
統 PCB 21%、其他電子 4%、FPD 3%。

主要客戶：台積電、日月光、臻鼎、力成、
Amkor、Micron、欣興、勝宏等 
主要競爭對手：EVG、TOK、SUSS、Yield 
Engineering Systems(YES)等

王彥鈞 stanley.wang@sinopac.com 
本次報告：座談會

志聖 (2467 TT) 
能見度高，獲利待驗證

永豐觀點

 投資評價與建議 
維持買進，目標價降至 358 元：(1) 3Q26 獲利表現不如預期，主因 PCB 比重拉升
及面對中國在地價格競爭，(2) 能見度高，但隨企業 AI Server 帶動 HDI/HLC PCB 
與 ABF 載板 2H26-2027 年接力放量，短期獲利改善有限。我們預估 2026/ 2027
年 EPS 為 14.01/19.92 元，目標 PE 則由 30x 下修至 18x，反映市場對設備類股評
價的調整，目標價隨之下修到 358 元(18x 2027 EPSF)。

### Page 2

2

志聖以光與熱為核心，發展熱處理平台：志聖成立於 1966 年，目前資本額 16.2 億
新台幣，為 G2C 集團當中的核心成員之一，以紫外光、烘烤、壓膜塗佈、電漿製
程等技術提供相關壓膜、貼、撕膜、烘烤、電鍍相關設備。公司初期以 PCB 設備
起家，而後伴隨台灣電子產業升級，切入半導體、組裝、面板、太陽能等產業，近
年陸續在 IC 載板、高階 HDI、先進封裝、HBM 等高附加價值市場發光發熱。公司
也與均豪、均華、東捷組成 G2C+聯盟，共同實現台灣先進封裝在地化。志聖主要
生產據點位於台灣林口、台中、中國廣州、昆山，並在台南、廣東、泰國、美國鳳
凰城有服務據點。1-3Q26 營收以產業別劃分，半導體佔約 45%、先進 PCB 28%、
傳統 PCB 21%、其他電子 4%、FPD 3%。主要競爭對手包含 EVG、TOK、SUSS、
Yield Engineering Systems(YES)等。客戶包含台積電、日月光、臻鼎、力成、
Amkor、Micron、欣興、勝宏等電子中上游廠商。 
3Q26 獲利不如預期：志聖 3Q26 營收 29.8 億(+7.0%QoQ，+93.9%YoY)，毛利
率 43.3%，稅後淨利 5.5 億(-13.4%QoQ，+162.1%YoY)，EPS 3.51 元。毛利率
表現不如預期主因出貨較多中國 PCB 用低階設備，使產品組合轉差，即半導體設
備/PCB 設備由 1H26 的 46%/46%變動至 1-3Q26 的 45%/49%，營業費用亦隨
獎酬增加及研發專案數的提升而高於預期。 
能見度高，但我們對獲利趨於審慎：管理層預期 2026 年底在手訂單約 100-110
億，較去年同期約 43 億元倍增，其中半導體佔比約 53%以上(以 OSAT 能見度較
高)、高階 PCB 35-37%。值得注意的是，半導體設備交期已由 4-10 個月拉長至 4-
13 個月；PCB 設備交期則約 3-8 個月，主因 OSAT 在廠房未完工前即下單，以及
上游零組件(機械手臂)交期的拉長所致，志聖持續以 G2C+聯盟和日系供應商溝通
並聯合採購，目前風險可控。展望後市，我們預期在晶圓廠先進封裝產能建置外溢
的環境下，志聖將透過挑單生產，降低傳統 PCB 接單比例以維持獲利率穩健成長。 
載板挖槽嵌入製程，2028 年再添動能：隨 AI 晶片尺寸持續放大，台系載板廠正
發展新的生產方式，以降低整片翹曲同時達到微縮線寬/線距的目的。志聖在此製
程中提供背面貼膜，以及 ABF 絕緣膜的壓合與撕膜設備。由於層數倍增將使壓合
與撕膜次數同步增加，加上載具尺寸放大推升單機價值，我們認為這將是志聖 
2028 年後載板設備的重要成長來源。 
財務預估：研究部預估志聖 2026 年營收 111.1 億元(+82.2%YoY)，毛利率 46.3%，
稅後淨利 22.7 億元(+173.0%YoY)，EPS 14.01 元(原估 16.94 元，下修主因本業
獲利表現不如預期及股本膨脹)。展望 2027 年，公司尚未給予明確展望，我們考量
短期 PCB/HLC 急單湧現壓抑毛利率，故下修獲利預估，預期 2027 年營收 148.4
億元(+33.5%YoY)，毛利率 46.9%，稅後淨利 32.2 億元(+42.2%YoY)，EPS 19.92
元(原估 25.15 元，下修主因獲利預估調降及股本膨脹)。

Document 147: 261117_daiwa_merida

Source PDF: all report pdf/261117_daiwa_merida.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-11-17
Pages: 4

### Page 1

Summary: Merida hosted its 3Q26 investor call on 17 November after market hours, 
following release of its 3Q26 results on 11 November (see our memo). Merida’s gross 
margin came in at 15.2% (vs. 19.8% in 2Q26; 13.7% in 3Q25), lower than our estimate 
(16.2%) and the consensus (17.3%). Factoring in the equity investment loss of 
TWD150m+, its 3Q26 EPS was TWD1.06, lower than our forecast (TWD1.68) and the 
consensus estimate (TWD1.65).

We have a Buy (1) rating with a 12-month TP of TWD96, based on a target PER of 17x 
applied on our 1-year-forward EPS forecast. For more information on this company, please 
refer to our latest flash note, 2Q26 review: mainly dragged by Specialized, on 14 August.

What’s the impact 
• 3Q26 results recap. Merida’s 3Q26 revenue of TWD6,098m (-12.1% QoQ, -15.6% 
YoY) missed our and the Bloomberg consensus estimates by 13.6% and 11.1%, 
respectively, which we attributed to the discount when destocking the older models. Its 
gross margin came in at 15.2% (vs. 19.8% in 2Q26; 13.7% in 3Q25), lower than our 
estimate (16.2%) and the consensus (17.3%), due to less OBM sales, the price discount 
for further inventory digestion and the depreciation of the Euro. Factoring in the equity 
investment loss of TWD150m+, its net profit of TWD176m (-44.8% QoQ, -71.9% YoY) 
missed our and the consensus estimates by 65.0% and 65.6%. Its 3Q26 EPS was 
TWD1.06, lower than our forecast (TWD1.68) and the consensus estimate (TWD1.65).  
• 2026 outlook update. Merida revised down its 2026 full-year shipments to 960k units 
(Taiwan plant 280k, down from previous 340k; China plant 680k, down from previous 
700k), as it continues to manage its existing model shipments and expects new models 
in 2027 to bring stronger demand. The company now sees recovery in Europe and 
China, with inventory digestion coming to an end: 9M26 sales in Europe grew 14% YoY, 
driven by strong demand for road bikes and gravel bikes; 9M 26 sales in China grew by 
1% while shipments grew by 7% (vs. revenue guidance of declining 15-20% at the 
beginning of 2026), driven by low- and mid-end models. 
• SBC expected to be profitable in 4Q26. Due to SBC’s conservative tax assumption 
(over 100% effective tax rate) in 1Q-3Q26, if and when the tax rate realised at year-end 
is determined to be less than currently allocated for, we expect there will be a huge profit 
recognised in 4Q26 to offset the estimated after-tax loss recognised in 1Q-3Q26. Merida 
and SBC will review the tax assumption policy after full-year results are out, as the 
policy has caused a great deal of investor confusion. Including USD15m of tariff refund 
in 3Q26, SBC’s pre-tax profit grew by 42% YoY for 9M26. Merida expects there is 
another USD5m of tariff refund to collect in 4Q26.

What we recommend 
We have a Buy (1) rating with a 12-month TP of TWD96, based on a target PER of 17x 
applied on our 1-year-forward EPS forecast. The stock is currently trading at PERs of 
13.2x/10.6x, based on our 2026/27E EPS, vs. its past-5-year one-year-forward PER 
trading range of 7-33x. Key downside risks: slower-than-expected e-bike penetration in the 
EU and the US, worse-than-expected demand in an inflationary environment and inventory 
digestion.

In the interests of timeliness, this document has not been edited.

   公司簡介 
日電貿成立於 1993 年，為台灣規模最大的被動元件代理商，其代理的企業品牌涵蓋
美國、日本與韓國等多家知名被動元件與電子零組件公司，產品線以各種電容、電感、
半導體元件與其他光電元件等為主，近期則開始聚焦於高規格電容類產品。

➢ 因業外減少，3Q26 EPS 降至 2.46 元，低於投研部預期 
3Q26 營收 58.61 億元，QoQ+13.7%、YoY+44.7%；受惠 AI 伺服器及
資料中心需求強勁，帶動 MLCC 等高價值產品出貨成長，毛利率提升至
21.12%，QoQ+3ppts，YoY+5.2ppts；EPS 2.49 元，獲利呈現季衰退
並低於投研部預期，主因為 2Q26 存在一筆業外股利收入 4.5 億元。 
➢ 4Q26 客戶放緩拉貨節奏，且大客戶佔比上升，營收與獲利續降 
受惠 AI 高階 MLCC 供需緊俏，以及日韓原廠產能轉移，部分消費性產
品交期已延長至 24～36 週，預期 4Q26 AI 與一般型產品報價同步調漲，
MLCC 營收占比可望由 52%升至 54%。預估 4Q26 營收 58.17 億元，
QoQ-0.8% 、 YoY+52.5% ；毛利率 20.64% ， QoQ-0.5ppts ，
YoY+2.7ppts；EPS 2.40 元。惟 2026 年底客戶控管庫存，加上大型客
戶議價能力較強，獲利率略受稀釋。 
➢ 供需失衡延續至 2027 年，漲價潮與 AI 占比破六成將使獲利再升 
日電貿因輝達 Vera Rubin 平台出貨遞延，客戶拉貨速度低於預期，導致
3Q26 存貨大幅攀升至 46.03 億元，主要反映高階元件 ASP 上升及長交
期產品備貨需求，惟隨 2027 年初 Rubin 放量，庫存可望逐步去化。展
望 2027 年，AI 與傳統伺服器需求同步成長，高階 MLCC 因製程難度
高、良率偏低，供給持續緊俏，預估 MLCC 營收占比升至 57%，年增
49.7%；固態電容及電解電容營收亦分別成長 44.7%及 34.9%。隨著 AI
營收占比提升至約 60%，加上原廠漲價及產品組合優化，毛利率有望重
拾上漲動能。 
➢ 預估 2027 年 EPS 14.37 元，給予買進評等，目標價 172 元 
投研部預估日電貿於 2026 年/2027 年營收 213.24 億元/304.43 億元，
YoY+35.58%/+42.76% ； 毛 利 率 19.99%/22.15% ， YoY+3.6ppts 
/2.2ppts；稅後 EPS 9.80 元/14.37 元，目前 PER 約在 13.5X/9.2X，參
考過去五年 PER 區間大部分在 10X~18X，目前股價以 2027 年衡量低
於區間下緣，考量公司獨家代理的多項高階產品因供需持續失衡而有望
漲價，使 AI 佔比持續上升，故給予買進評等，但下修目標價至 172 元
(2027F PE 12X)。

- 2 -

個股報告

3Q26 因業外減少，EPS 降至 2.49 元，低於投研部預期 
日電貿 3Q26 營收 58.61 億元，QoQ+13.7%、YoY+44.7%，主因為 AI 伺服器、資
料中心及電源相關需求同步增加，帶動陶瓷電容(MLCC)等高附加價值產品出貨成長，
以營收結構來看，公司三大類別分別為 MLCC 佔 52%、固態電容(SCP)佔 26%、電解
電容(ECP)佔 9%。此外，SCP 佔比下滑至 26%，因客戶擔憂缺貨而於 2Q26 提前備
貨，加上 3Q26 MLCC 成長速度更快所致；毛利率提升至 21.12%，QoQ+3ppts，
YoY+5.2ppts，呈現出 AI 伺服器與資料中心需求強勁，帶動 MLCC 與各類電容產品
出貨上升的現象；然而稅前淨利 8.98 億元，QoQ-13.3%，主因為 2Q26 存在一筆業
外股利收入 4.5 億元；EPS 2.49 元，QoQ-22.4%，YoY+26.4%。3Q26 營收低於投
研部預期，因公司客戶於 2Q26 提前備貨，故 3Q26 季增幅回歸較穩定狀態；獲利亦
因上述的業外減少而低於預期。累計前三季 EPS 7.4 元，YoY+76.2%。 
圖 1：日電貿產品組合佔比預估

資料來源：國票投顧整理 
2026 年底客戶放緩拉貨節奏，且大客戶佔比上升，4Q26 營收與獲利續降 
日電貿目前因 AI 用的高階 MLCC 供需緊俏，一般型產品也受到日韓原廠產能轉移影
響，部分消費性產品交期已拉長至 24~36 週，預期 4Q26 公司旗下 AI 與一般型料號
報價將調漲，帶動相關營收隨之上升，因此 MLCC 比重有望由 52%進一步升至 54%。
投研部預估 4Q26 營收 58.17 億元，QoQ-0.8%，YoY+52.5%，季衰退的原因推測為
公司客戶於 2026 年底進行盤點並控管庫存，故傾向放緩拉貨腳步；毛利率 20.64%，
QoQ-0.5ppts，YoY+2.7ppts，儘管 AI 占比將超過 50%，但大型客戶占比也持續增
加，公司對這些客戶的產品議價與通路利潤率相對較低，故毛利率有所稀釋；營業利
益率 13.97%，QoQ-0.85ppts，YoY+3.2ppts；稅後 EPS 2.40 元，QoQ-3.5%，
YoY+89.3%。 
MLCC 供需失衡延續至 2027 年，漲價潮與 AI 占比破六成將使獲利再升 
目前輝達(NVDA.US) Vera Rubin 平台出貨遞延，使日電貿的客戶拉貨速度低於預期，
因此在 3Q26 季底累積的庫存金額高達 46.03 億元，且高階元件 ASP 上升，再加上
這些產品的交期較長而需建立更安全的庫存水位，因此相較過往才會異常飆高。公司
認為未來一至兩季需求仍佳，待 Rubin 於 2027 年初開始大量拉貨後，庫存水位可望
下降並轉化為營收，同時存貨週轉天數也從目前的 86 天逐季下降。 
過去兩年，AI 伺服器市場快速成長，傳統伺服器需求則相對溫和；然而，隨著企業 IT

### Page 3

- 3 -

個股報告 
支出回溫與 CSP 大廠持續擴大資本支出，預期自 2026 年~2027 年，傳統伺服器與
AI 伺服器將同步成長，帶動整體伺服器產業規模持續擴大。就市場規模而言，全球伺
服器於 2027 年預估約達 1.1 兆美元，並於 2028 年再提升至 1.6 兆美元，年增幅約
15%。其中，輝達 NVL72 機櫃將成為重要成長動能，2028 年相關市場產值可達約 1
兆美元，占整體市場約 63%；另一方面，ASIC 伺服器近年出貨量快速增加，預估至
2028 年市場滲透率將提升至 65%，顯示雲端業者正逐步擴大自研晶片的部署。從晶
片出貨量來看，2025 年 ASIC 約為 430 萬顆，輝達與 AMD 相關 GPU 則約 550 萬
顆；至 2028 年，ASIC 出貨量預估將增至 2,220 萬顆，較 2025 年成長約 5.2 倍，
GPU 相關出貨量則可達 1,610 萬顆，成長約 2.9 倍。整體而言，未來伺服器市場將由
傳統伺服器復甦、AI 訓練與推論需求擴張，以及 ASIC 自研晶片加速滲透等多重因素
共同推動，顯示 GPU 與 ASIC 兩大運算架構將呈現同步成長，而非單純的替代關係。 
圖 2：日電貿存貨金額 單位：億元

資料來源：國票投顧整理 
AI 伺服器所需的高階 MLCC，因產品規格涉及高容值、高耐壓與高可靠度要求，製程
難度明顯高於一般規格產品，導致產品良率相對偏低。相較一般 MLCC 良率可達
99.9%，部分 AI 高階產品良率依產品別與不同客戶的設計差異約落在 40%~80%不
等，使生產相同數量的合格品，需投入更多製造資源，因此形成較大的產能消耗。儘
管如此，受惠 AI 伺服器與高效能運算需求持續擴張，高階 MLCC 訂單仍相當充足，
供給短期內不易快速增加。日電貿目前客戶需求都維持強勁，尤其 MLCC 產品拉貨動
能明確，因此維持緊俏的狀況將延續至 2027 年，使其成為被動元件產業中具備較高
成長潛力的重要產品。投研部預估 MLCC 佔比將自 2026 年底的 54%上升至 2027 年
的 57%，貢獻營收 YoY+49.7%；固態電容與電解電容雖然分別由 25%下滑至 24%與
9%下滑至 8%，但以貢獻營收衡量，YoY 仍分別增長 44.7%與 34.91%。儘管通路商
的毛利率較其他被動元件製造廠的毛利低，但在 2027 年預期 AI 占比將可望達 60%，
因此仍有望藉由調整旗下客戶去優化產品組合，且代理的原廠漲價也有機會帶動通路
價差增加，故 2027 年毛利率有望再次上揚。 
預估 2027 年 EPS 14.37 元，給予買進評等，目標價 172 元 
投 研 部 預 估 日 電 貿 2026 年 /2027 年 營 收 213.24 億元/304.43 億 元 ，
YoY+35.58%/+42.76%；毛利率為 19.99%/22.15%，YoY+3.6ppts/2.2ppts；稅後
EPS 9.80 元/14.37 元，目前 PER 約在 13.7X/9.2X，參考過去五年 PER 區間大部分在
10X~18X，目前股價以 2027 年衡量低於區間下緣，考量公司獨家代理的多項高階產
品因供需持續失衡而有望漲價，使 AI 佔比持續上升，故給予買進評等，但下修目標價
至 172 元(2027F PE 12X)。

研 究 報 告                
穩懋(3105.TT) 
產業：電子–半導體 
2027 光通訊跟衛星本業雙引擎 認列聯茂收益業外動能強勁 
 投資評價與建議 
公司過往Forward PER落在20~30倍，考量光通訊與衛星業務出貨放量，毛利
率迎來結構性跳升，有利估值重估，維持「增加持股」評等。 
 重點評論分析 
3Q26營收符合預期，毛利率因產品組合優化上升至37.4%：3Q26營收58.6億
元，季增11%，營收符合預期主要係因傳統消費性電子旺季，加上航太衛星與
光電產品動能強勁，毛利率37.4%，季增9.2個百分點，主要係因產品組合優化，
航太衛星與光電產品營收占比過半，加上產能利用率上升至75%，業外收入
6.98億元，主因聯茂貢獻業外EPS約1.61元，稅後EPS 4.55元。 
4Q26營運淡季不淡，2027成⻑動能聚焦Optical跟Infrastructure：(1)4Q26
面臨手機傳統淡季與年底例行性歲修，但受惠於PD產能持續開出與其他產品
線營收持平至微幅成⻑，預估4Q26營收將季增1~3%，毛利率37~39%。(2)光
通訊PD於3Q26逐步放量，4Q26將持續成⻑，預估2027年大幅放量，CW Laser
正進行可靠度測試，預估2027年小量貢獻，顯著營收爆發期落在2028年，EML
處於產品開發與測試階段，2028年才有機會產生營收貢獻。(3)航太衛星帶動
Infrastructure成為3Q26季增幅度最高的產品線，衛星佔Infrastructure營收
比重已升至40%，超越傳統基地台。(4)預估聯茂2027年EPS 72.22元，穩懋採
權益法認列持股23%之投資收益約61.7億元，貢獻EPS約14.56元。 
 投資風險 
光通PD跟雷射放量不如預期、衛星發射遞延。

### Page 2

2 
| 營運概況 
3Q26 產品組合優化帶動毛利率與 EPS 創高：3Q26 營收 58.6 億元，季增 11%，營收符合預期主要係因傳統消費
性電子旺季，加上航太衛星與光電產品動能強勁，毛利率 37.4%，季增 9.2 個百分點，優於財測，主要係因產品
組合優化，航太衛星與光電產品營收占比過半，加上產能利用率上升至 75%，營業利益率 24.2%，業外收入 6.98
億元，因持有聯茂 23%股權並採權益法認列投資收益，聯茂貢獻業外 EPS 約 1.61 元，稅後 EPS 4.55 元。 
4Q26 營運淡季不淡，2027 成⻑動能聚焦 Optical 跟 Infrastructure：(1)儘管 4Q26 面臨手機傳統淡季與年底例
行性歲修，但受惠於 PD 產能持續開出與其他產品線營收持平至微幅成⻑，預估 4Q26 營收將季增 1~3%，毛利
率 37~39%，主要受惠產品組合優化跟產能利用率維持 70%以上。(2)中階手機受臨中國 PA 供應鏈本土化及記憶
體缺貨的逆風，但穩懋聚焦中高階手機市場，動能維持穩健，4Q26 Cellular 營收僅呈現小幅下滑，WiFi 產品在
Router 需求回流下，3Q26 雙位數成⻑，4Q26 呈現持平至微幅成⻑。(3)光通訊 PD 於 3Q26 逐步放量，4Q26
將持續成⻑，預估 2027 年產能完全開出並大幅放量，CW Laser 正進行可靠度測試，預估 2027 年小量貢獻，顯
著營收爆發期落在 2028，EML 處於產品開發與測試階段，2028 年才有機會產生營收貢獻。(4)航太衛星帶動
Infrastructure 成為 3Q26 季增幅度最高的產品線，衛星佔 Infrastructure 營收比重已升至 40%，超越傳統基地
台，目前 GaN 月產能是 300 片左右，公司積極擴充 GaN 產能，預計 1Q27 導入 GaN 6 吋製程生產。(5)聯茂
2027 年爆發性成⻑，預估聯茂 2027 年 EPS 72.22 元，穩懋採權益法認列持股 23%之投資收益約 61.7 億元，貢
獻EPS約14.56元。

| 公 司 簡 介 
穩懋(3105)為全球最大的化合物半導體晶圓代工廠，核心業務聚焦於微波通訊與光電元件的砷化鎵(GaAs)及氮化
鎵(GaN)晶圓代工服務，公司總部及研發中心設於台灣桃園龜山(華亞科技園區)，主要六吋晶圓生產基地位於桃
園華亞廠及龜山廠，總產能約 4.3 萬片，3Q26 產能利用率為 75%。3Q26 年營收比重為 Cellular(手機
PA)25~30%、Infrastructure(通訊基礎建設)35~40%、Wi-Fi 15~20%、Optical 10~15%、其他 3%。主要客
戶包含 Broadcom、Lumentum、Murata 等國際大廠，銷售地區為台灣 8%、亞洲 70%、美洲 15%、歐洲
7%。 
穩懋的微波通訊產品處理高頻訊號的放大與切換，主要元件為異質接面雙載子電晶體(HBT)與假晶高電子遷移率
電晶體(pHEMT) ，用於智慧型手機功率放大器(PA)、Wi-Fi 路由器及低軌衛星設備的射頻(RF)訊號，提供低雜

營收走勢圖

研究員/丁宥霖 yulin.ting@uni-psg.com

貿聯 3Q26 財 報 優 於 預 期 ， 營 業 利 益 率 創 歷 史 新 高 ， 加 上 
Interplex 併表效益優於原先預估，為後續營運增添新亮點。展
望未來，研究部持續看好貿聯中長期成長潛力，主要考量：(1) 隨 
AI 機櫃功耗持續提升，電力傳輸規格朝高電流、高壓化發展，帶
動 Power Whip、Busbar 等產品升級，單櫃電源產值可望倍增；
(2) 高速互連朝 1.6T 以上演進，AEC 與高階光纖業務接續放
量，推升資料傳輸產值；(3) Interplex 併購補足精密機構能力，
結合既有電力及高速互連布局，貿聯可望由零組件供應商逐步延
伸至機櫃次系統整合，擴大單一客戶供應範圍及長期成長商機。
研究部將 Interplex 納入預估，上修 2026、2027 年稀釋後 EPS 
至 73.49 元（+67.4% YoY，原估 68.77 元）及 145.24 元
（+103.2% YoY，原估 117.82 元）。惟考量近期 AI 資料中心建
置不確定性升高，可能使新平台導入與機櫃出貨時程遞延，壓抑
評價表現，研究部將本益比由 25 倍下修至 20 倍，以 2027 年 
EPS 為評價基礎，目標價 2,950 元，投資評等由「買進」調升至
「強力買進」。

重點摘要： 
1. 3Q26 營業利益率創歷史新高，財報優於預期 
貿聯 3Q26 營收 278.75 億元（+19.7% QoQ、+51.4% YoY），符
合預期。其中 HPC 營收成長強勁（+30% QoQ、+61% YoY），營收
占比由 2Q26 的 42% 提升至 46%。隨 AEC 拉貨恢復正常，上半
年產線未達經濟規模的不利影響逐步消除，帶動整體毛利率回升
至 32.53%（+1.80ppt QoQ、−0.72ppt YoY）；加上營收規模擴
大，營業費用率降至 11.75%（−1.76ppt QoQ、−1.97ppt YoY），
推升營業利益率至 20.78%（+3.56ppt QoQ、+1.24ppt YoY），創
歷史新高。營業利益達 57.92 億元（+44.5% QoQ、+61.0% YoY），
優於原估的 52.50 億元。業外因匯兌損失及利息費用合計損失 
2.30 億元，稅後淨利達 42.90 億元（+43.9% QoQ、+63.2% YoY），
優於原估的 39.78 億元，稀釋後 EPS 為 20.34 元，優於原估
的 19.45 元。

### Page 2

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  2

2. Interplex 併表貢獻超預期，上修 4Q26 營收與獲利 
貿聯 10 月營收 118.46 億元（+26.9% MoM、+80.0% YoY），創
歷史新高，主要受惠 Interplex 於 9 月底完成交割，10 月首
度完整併表即貢獻營收 6,700 萬美元，約為併購公告時近 12 個
月平均月營收的一倍。Interplex 為 NVIDIA NVLink Switch 機
殼主要供應商，受惠 AI 機櫃需求強勁，併表貢獻高於原先預估。
考量併購效益優於預期，加上既有業務持續成長，研究部上修 
4Q26 營收至 349.76 億元（+25.5% QoQ、+76.8% YoY，原估 
302.69 億元）。預估毛利率為 32.49%（−0.04ppt QoQ、+0.56ppt 
YoY），大致持平前季；隨營收規模擴大及 Interplex 較低費用
率貢獻，營業費用率降至 10.85%，推升營業利益率至 21.64%
（+0.86ppt QoQ、+3.27ppt YoY，原估 20.0%）。預估 4Q26 稅
後淨利達 55.28 億元（+28.9% QoQ、+101.8% YoY），稀釋後 EPS 
由原估 22.38 元上修至 26.21 元（ +28.9% QoQ、+101.8% YoY）。

3. AI 機櫃供電架構升級，電源單櫃產值與市占率同步提升 
隨 AI 伺服器運算密度持續提高，機櫃功耗由 GB200 世代約 
120kW，朝 Vera Rubin 世代 200kW 以上發展，電力傳輸規格同
步升級。在既有低壓直流架構下，功耗提升帶動電流需求增加，
Power Whip 與 Busbar 須升級至更高載流規格，推升單櫃電源
產值；隨機櫃功率持續攀升，傳統 54VDC 架構面臨銅材用量、配
電損耗及機櫃空間限制，NVIDIA 已推動 800VDC 高壓直流架構，
透過提高配電電壓、減少電力轉換環節，支援未來 MW 級 AI 機
櫃需求。貿聯除受惠電源產品規格升級，於 Vera Rubin 世代的 
Busbar 市占率亦可望明顯提升，推動單櫃電源產值較 GB 世代
倍增。考量新平台相關出貨預計於 1H27 起逐季放量，研究部預
估 2027 年電源營收成長約 114% YoY；中長期隨 800VDC 架構
逐步導入，既有 Power Whip、Busbar 及高電流連接技術可望延
伸至新一代配電系統，持續擴大供應商機。

### Page 3

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  3

4. 高速互連升級至 1.6T，AEC 與高階光纖雙軌成長 
隨 AI 資料中心傳輸頻寬由 800G 朝 1.6T 以上升級，高速互連
對訊號完整性、傳輸距離及功耗的要求持續提高。短距離傳輸仍以
銅纜具成本優勢，較長距離則由光纖滿足頻寬與訊號傳輸需求，預
期 AEC 與光互連將持續並存。AEC 方面，1.6T 新規格因材料成本
及組裝難度提高，產品單價與毛利率可望優於前代，預計於 4Q26 
至 1Q27 逐步導入量產，研究部預估 2027 年 AEC 營收成長約 
41% YoY，主要受惠高階產品占比提升。光纖方面，新富生主要供
應 AI 資料中心機櫃間傳輸所需的 MPO、MT 高密度光纖跳線及連
接器組件，受惠高速光纖互連需求成長，月營收較 2026 年初成長
逾一倍，獲利同步改善。中長期來看，貿聯亦積極布局 Optical 
Shuffle、CPO 交換器方案及 Micro-VCSEL 光互連技術，逐步由傳
統線纜組裝延伸至高階光學組件，為後續成長開拓更多商機。

圖一、貿聯-KY 1Q25～4Q27F HPC 營收結構

資料來源：統一投顧預估

5. 半導體設備需求強勁，非 HPC 業務維持穩健 
貿聯 3Q26 半導體設備營收成長 11% QoQ、70% YoY，為非 HPC 業
務主要成長動能。隨先進製程及半導體設備投資持續推進，客戶需
求由傳統線束延伸至設備模組、精密組裝及次系統整合，帶動單機
供應產值提升。貿聯已深入薄膜沉積與蝕刻設備供應鏈，並逐步拓
展至微影及檢測設備，擴大產品應用範圍。其他業務方面，工廠自
動化、醫療、能源及車用等終端需求維持穩健，提供多元營收來源。

### Page 4

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  4

6. 上修 2026～2028 年獲利預估，營運槓桿與併購效益持續擴
大 
研究部將 Interplex 併表貢獻納入預估，上修貿聯 2026 年營收
至 1,069.96 億元（+50.2% YoY，原估 1,021.18 億元），毛利率
估 31.4%，稀釋後 EPS 73.49 元（+67.4% YoY，原估 68.77 元）。
展望 2027 年，受惠 AI 電源及高速互連業務持續成長，加上 
Interplex 全年併表，預估營收達 1,710.84 億元（+59.9% YoY，
原估 1,517.46 億元）。隨高毛利 HPC 業務占比提升，毛利率可
望升至 34.3%（原估 34.0%）；加上營收規模擴大及 Interplex 
較低費用率貢獻，營業費用率降至 10.2%（原估 13.0%），推升營
業利益率至 24.1%（原估 21.0%），稀釋後 EPS 上修至 145.24 
元（+103.2% YoY，原估 117.82 元）。展望 2028 年，隨 AI 機
櫃供電架構朝 800VDC 演進、高速互連規格持續升級，加上 
Interplex 受惠機櫃需求成長及精密機構件供應範圍擴大，預期將
共同帶動營運成長。研究部預估營收達 2,136.69 億元（+24.9% 
YoY），稀釋後 EPS 196.20 元（+35.1% YoY，原估 171.03 元）。

圖二、貿聯-KY 2025～2028F 各應用別營收占比

資料來源：公司資料及統一投顧預估

7. 中長期成長趨勢明確，投資評等調升至「強力買進」 
研究部持續看好貿聯中長期成長潛力，隨 AI 機櫃電力與高速互
連規格持續升級，帶動單櫃供應產值提升，加上 Interplex 補足
精密機構能力，逐步擴大機櫃次系統供應範圍，有利深化客戶合作
關係並提升市占率。惟考量近期 AI 資料中心建置不確定性升高，
可能使新平台導入及機櫃出貨時程遞延，壓抑市場評價，研究部將
本益比由 25 倍下修至 20 倍。以 2027 年稀釋後 EPS 145.24 
元為評價基礎，目標價 2,950 元，投資評等由「買進」調升至「強
力買進」。

告 中 所 有 的 意 見 及 預 估 ， 如 有 變 更 恕 不 另 行 通 知 。

供 專 屬 之 投 資 建 議 。 文 中 所 載 資 訊 或 任 何 意 見 ， 不 構 成 任 何 買 賣 有 價 證 券 或 其 他 投
資 標 的 之 要 約 、 宣 傳 或 引 誘 等 事 項 。 對 於 本 投 資 報 告 所 討 論 或 建 議 之 任 何 證 券 、 投
資 標 的 ， 或 文 中 所 討 論 或 建 議 之 投 資 策 略 ， 投 資 人 應 就 其 是 否 適 合 本 身 財 務 狀 況 與

陳 述 或 保 證 。 本 投 資 報 告 載 述 意 見 進 行 更 改 與 撤 回 並 不 另 行 通 知 。 本 投 資 報 告 並 非

4576 大銀微(HMC)

報告日期：2026/11/12 研 究 員：洪 國 峰 (Titan Horng)  
壹、訪 談／法 說 內 容 
 在半導體先進封裝、AI 及矽光子等高階應用需求暢旺，其中
微米與奈米級定位系統交期 5~6 個月，精密運動及控制元件交
期約 3~4 個月，呈現供不應求。 
 受惠於晶圓大廠持續擴充先進製程、後段設備廠訂單持續成
長，大銀微在超高精度平台訂單已經看到 2027 年年中，另外
搭配新產能將於 2Q27 投產。預估 2027 年微米與奈米級定位
系統營收金額 31.5 億元，YoY+28.0%。 
一. 公告 3Q26 稅後 EPS 2.14 元 
大銀微 3Q26 營收金額 12.18 億元，YoY+76.73%，QoQ+19.01%，
營業利益 2.96 億元，YoY+312.6%，QoQ+61.12%，稅後盈餘 2.57
億元，YoY+255.8%，QoQ+123.72%，稅後 EPS 2.14 元。 
二. 預估 4Q26 稅後 EPS 2.44 元 
大銀微 10/2026 營收金額 4.10 億元，YoY+68.75%，MoM-3.96%。
在訂單呈現滿載水準，預估大銀微 4Q26 營收金額 12.84 億元，
YoY+69.94% ， QoQ+5.37% ，預估營業利益 3.32 億元，
YoY+232.13% ， QoQ+12.21% ，預估稅後盈餘 2.92 億元，
YoY+223.25%，QoQ+13.78%，預估稅後 EPS 2.44 元。 
三. 預估 2026 年稅後 EPS 6.50 元 
大銀微 2026 年在半導體、PCB 與矽光子檢測裝置需求延續，帶
動微米奈米級高精度定位系統訂單維持數個月的能見度。另外中
國大陸工具機谷底復甦，推升控制元件產品需求，帶動大銀微 2026
年營運成長。預估大銀微 2026 年營收 43.8 億元，YoY+61.37%，
預估稅後盈餘 7.79 億元，YoY+222.63%，預估稅後 EPS 6.50 元。 
四. 預估 2027 年稅後 EPS 9.00 元 
大銀微 2027 年在推動的 One HIWIN Solution 單一零組件供應
商，往次系統、完整定位平台以及共同開發解決方案的角色移動，
搭配同集團上銀資源整合，將有助於大銀微產品附加價值提升。
宏遠預估大銀微 2027 年營收金額 53.00 億元，YoY+21.00%，預
估稅後盈餘 10.79 億元，YoY+38.52%，預估稅後 EPS 9.00 元。 
五. 投資建議買進，目標價 252 元 
觀察國內傳動元件同業 2027 年營收成長率亞德客 ROE 21%，營
收成長率 10%，PER 15X ；上銀 ROE 11% ，營收成長率 16%，
PER 22X，大銀微 ROE 21%，營收成長率 21%，PER 21.6X，以
ROE 與營收成長率相較同業來的佳，評價與同業相較仍在低檔水
準，故投資建議買進，目標價 252 元（28X 2027 年稅後 EPS）

貳、產 業 及 營 運 概 況

一.公司簡介 
大銀微系統股份有限公司創立於 1997 年 4 月，資本額 11.98 億
元，並於 2019 年 9 月正式掛牌上市。大銀微行屬於上銀集團旗
下的核心關係企業。大銀微聚焦精準定位與高性能驅動控制技
術，並以機電整合、產品製造及技術服務之整體能力作為核心競
爭優勢，深耕半導體、電子生產設備、智慧自動化及工具機等領
域。受惠半導體先進製程與封裝需求成長，大銀微以領先業界之
精密定位技術及系統整合方案擴大服務範圍，針對客戶需求提供
專業精密定位系統整合方案，透過技術互補與研發合作，協助全 
球頂尖半導體客戶縮短開發週期並提升產出效益。

二. 2026 年營運概況及 2027 年展望 
大銀行營收主要有精密運動及控制元件、微米與奈米級定位系統
與其他三部分。

預估 2027 年精密運動及控制元件營收金額 21.00 億元，
YoY+11.7%

精密運動及控制元件產品線包括：線性馬達、直驅馬達、真空馬
達、力矩馬達、伺服馬達、高速主軸馬達、位置量測系統等，並
搭配自行開發的驅動器及控制器，達奈米級高精度定位控制功
能。廣泛應用於半導體設備/自動化搬送/FPD 設備/PCB 設備
/AOI 檢測設備/SMT 設備/雷射加工器/各式機器手臂/工具機/電
動車相關/太陽能等不同產業。精密運動及控制元件 2025 年營收
金額 12.64 億元，YoY+3.1%。

精密運動及控制元件 1Q~3Q26 營收金額 13.83 億元，
YoY+45.43%。在中國大陸對驅動器需求高，目前處於供不應求狀
態，帶動包括線性馬達、直驅馬達、伺服馬達產能稼動率在高檔
水準，預估 2026 年精密運動及控制元件產品營收金額 18.80 億
元，YoY+48.7%。

預期 2027 年，在中國大陸工具機出口額與金屬切削設備產業仍呈
現正向發展，整體需求大於供給，訂單能見度己達 3~4 個月，預
估 2027 年精密運動及控制元件產品營收金額 21.00 億元，
YoY+11.7%。

微米與奈米級定位系統產品線包括：各式高精密定位平台，如單
軸、X-Y 雙軸、橋式、龍門、空氣軸承、多維度定位平台，可滿

雷射加工、膠材塗佈、金屬加工等。微米與奈米級定位系統 2025
年營收金額 14.27 億元，YoY+41.8%。

微米與奈米級定位系統 1Q~3Q26 營收金額 16.8 億元，
YoY+69.5%。在 AI 產業熱潮帶動半導體產業投資本支出持續加
速，間接推升微米與奈米級定位系統需求，其中半導體定位平台
能見度約 5~6 個月，超高精度平台甚至可看到 2027 年中，在此
熱潮帶動下，預估 2026 年微米與奈米級定位系統營收金額 24.60
億元，YoY+72.4%。

預期 2027 年，受惠於晶圓大廠持續擴充先進製程、後段設備廠訂
單持續成長，大銀微在超高精度平台訂單已經看到 2027 年年中，
包括 CoWoP、CoPoS、FoPLP 都持續配合客戶打樣中，另外搭
配新產能將於 2Q27 投 產。預 估 2027 年微米與奈米級定位系統營
收金額 31.5 億元，YoY+28.0%。

圖四、在半導體製程演進，持續往高階設備升級，提升平台方案價值

資料來源：公司&宏遠投顧彙整

大銀微 2026 年在半導體、PCB 與矽光子檢測裝置需求延續，帶
動微米奈米級高精度定位系統訂單維持數個月的能見度。另外中
國大陸工具機產業谷底復甦，推升控制元件等產品需求，帶動大
銀微 2026 年營運呈現大幅成長。宏遠預估大銀微 2026 年營收金
額 43.80 億元，YoY+61.37% ，預估營業利益 9.53 億元，

稅後 EPS 6.50 元。

預估 2027 年稅後盈餘 10.79 億元，YoY+38.52%，稅 後 EPS 9.00
元

大銀微 2027 年在推動的 One HIWIN Solution 單一零組件供應
商，往次系統、完整定位平台以及共同開發解決方案的角色移動，
搭配同集團上銀資源整合，將有助於大銀微產品附加價值提升。
宏遠預估大銀微 2027 年營收金額 53.00 億元，YoY+21.00%，預
估營業利益 13.86 億元，YoY+45.4%，預估稅後盈餘 10.79 億元，
YoY+38.52%，預估稅後 EPS 9.00 元。

### Page 1

www.imeritz.com

두산에너빌리티 (034020)

Company Brief 
유틸리티/건설 
Analyst 문경원, CFA 
kyeongwon.moon@meritz.co.kr 
RA 이동석 
dongseok.lee@meritz.co.kr 3Q26 Review: 바닥을 잡는다면 지금  
믹스 개선에 따른 이익률 개선 조금씩 관찰 중 
3Q26 연결 영업이익은 2,612억원(+90.6% YoY)을 기록하며 컨센서스(2,455억원)
를 소폭 상회했다. 밥캣(1,674억원)이 컨센서스(1,761억원)를 소폭 하회한 가운데, 
에너빌리티 부문 영업이익(+179% YoY, OPM 6.6%)이 호실적을 이끌었다. 환율 하
락에도 불구, 가스터빈, 원전 등 고수익성 프로젝트의 비중 상승이 주요 원인이다. 
3Q26 누적 수주는 10.0조원(+85.5% YoY)을 기록했다. 4Q26 가스복합 EPC 수주 
등을 감안하면 연간 가이던스(13.3조원) 달성은 무난하다. 한수원과의 협상 결렬로 
이목이 집중된 체코 두코바니 시공 수주는 2027년 인식될 확률이 높아 보인다. 주간
사가 바뀌더라도 이미 프로젝트에 참여 중인 동사를 배제하기는 쉽지 않다.

대미투자 이후, 연말~연초 수주 모멘텀에 집중 
연말 연초에 다음과 수주 모멘텀이 집중되면서, 주가 상승을 이끌 전망이다. 
1) 대형원전: 정부 자금 중심의 Westinghouse LLI(장납기 기자재) 발주가 몰린다. 
우선 미국 DOE(에너지부)가 제공한 대출을 기반으로 미국 수주가 기대된다.  폴란드 
정부 역시 최근 LLI 발주 작업을 시작했다. 한편, 한국 정부는 연말까지 100억 달러 
규모의 대미 투자 송금을 협의 중인데, 이 역시 DOC(상무부) 주도의 대형 원전 건설
을 위한 LLI 발주에 쓰일 가능성이 높다 
2) SMR: 오래 기다려왔던 Nuscale 향 수주가 시작되고 있다. 우선 29일 모듈 1기 
제작 계약을 체결했다. 현금이 부족한 Nuscale이 용기를 냈다는 건, 곧 Nuscale-
TVA 간 PPA 가 임박했음을 의미한다. 본격적인 수주는 PPA 직후 예상된다.  
3) 가스 터빈: 2026년 초 해외 수주(7기)는 2029년까지 인도 계획이다. 이후 2030
년 슬랏 예약을 완료하고 2031년 물량을 논의 중으로 보인다. 지금은 2030년 슬랏
에 대한 PO가 나와 수주잔고에 인식할 타이밍이다. 역시 수출 물량으로 추측한다.

성장에 대한 기대가 사라진 밸류에이션, 지금이 바닥 
투자의견 Buy를 유지, 할인율 상승을 반영하여 적정주가를 109,000원으로 -17% 
하향 조정한다. 금리 상승, 환율 하락, 트럼프 지지율 하락 등 Top-down 리스크에 
최근 조정을 받았지만, 추가적인 주가 하락 폭은 제한적일 전망이다. EV/선행 수주잔
고 지표는 현재 1.0배 수준까지 하락하며, 원전의 성장성이 주목받지 못했던 
2021~2024년 수준에 근접하게 내려왔다.  
 Meritz Research  2026. 10. 30

Buy (20거래일 평균종가 대비 상승 여력 기준)

STRONG RESILIENCE TO EXTERNAL SHOCKS
, 
SOLID 
MACROECONOMIC FUNDAMENTALS 
◼ 
 Over the past 
 two years
 , India has demonstrated remarkable resilience in the face of external shocks, while its 
macroeconomic fundamentals have remained solid.  
◼ 
 In FY2025/2026  
(ended in March 2026)
 , India was hit by a sharp rise in US tariffs. However, thanks to supportive fiscal 
and monetary policies, 
 real GDP  
grew by 7.8% in FY2026, placing India among the fastest
 -
 growing Asian economies.  
◼ 
 For the current fiscal year, the Indian economy is facing rising energy prices 
 (
due to 
 the conflict in the Middle East and 
increased food prices 
 because 
 of 
an 
unfavourable monsoon
 ) 
as well as 
 monetary tightening. Nevertheless, despite these 
new challenges, real GDP growth is expected to exceed 7%.  
◼ 
 India is currently in a stronger position than it was in 2022 to cope with the new energy shock. The fiscal leeway to support  
the economy has increased with the decline in the fiscal deficit, and inflation is more contained.  
◼ 
 Although 
 rising  
energy prices have put pressure on external accounts, FX reserves 
 remain  
sufficient to cover the country’s 
short
 -
 term external financing needs. 
 Yet, 
downward pressures on the rupee have been 
 significant 
 and 
could persist in the 
short term
 . 
In 
H1 
2026, India’s external accounts withstood the rise in energy prices fairly well
 , notably  
thanks to the sharp increase 
in workers’ remittances. 
 T
he 
widening of the trade deficit was primarily due to the substantial increase in machinery and 
equipment imports 
 while t
 he oil trade deficit increased 
 only 
moderately, as the rise in energy prices was offset by a decline 
in import volumes. 
 However, 
in H2 2026, the oil trade deficit is expected to widen significantly.  
Additionally, large portfolio outflows have exacerbated downward pressure on the rupee, necessitating interventions by 
the central bank in foreign exchange markets and the adoption of regulatory measures to support the currency. Although 
these measures hav
 e led to a rebound in the rupee (at least temporarily), inflationary pressures, volatile portfolio 
investments, and low net foreign direct investments are 
 expected to continue 
 to put pressure on the currency.  
◼ 
 D
ownward pressures on the rupee 
 and 
 rising bond yields will reduce the government's 
 already limited 
 fiscal capacity to 
increase public investment and 
 support economic development. However, this will not undermine its ability to refinance 
its debt, given its safe structure.  
◼ 
 T
he banking sector is sufficiently robust to cope with the expected rise in credit risks.

STRONG ECONOMIC GROWTH DESPITE 
STRUCTURAL CONSTRAINTS 
◼ 
 In October 2026, the IMF revised upward 
 India’s  
medium
 -
 term growth 
 forecast 
 from 6.5% to 6.8%  
per year
 . Nonetheless, 
to achieve such strong growth performance, 
 India will have to adapt to the challenges posed by artificial intelligence  
(AI)
, 
while its infrastructure and education system are not prepared for it.  
◼ 
 Moreover, India’s development remains highly uneven. 
 The middle class is still modest
 . This is explained by a large 
informal economy, heavy concentration of employment in low value
 -
 added sectors such as agriculture, low participation 
rates among women and youth, and limited industrial development. All this, in turn, reflects India’s weak i
 ntegration into 
global value chains.  
◼ 
 India faces two key structural challenges: i
 ) adapting the services sector to AI and ii) accelerating industrial development. 
Even with progress in sectors such as smartphones and pharmaceuticals, India’s industrial base remains too small to 
absorb its vast labour force. 
 Labo
u
r market 
 rigidities  
and the mismatch between training and business needs are holding 
back both the creation of quality jobs and industrial development.  
◼ 
 Low net foreign direct investment is a 
 major  
 obstacle. While 
 India attracts 
 new 
 FDI, retention is weak
 : 
 despite 
improvements in logistics, governance issues and regulatory barriers have 
 led 
 disinvestments to triple over the past five 
years.  
◼ 
 India 
 may fail in completing its 
 transition to a higher
 -
 value, more industrialised and inclusive economy if reforms do not 
accelerate 
 significantly
 . Structural change
 s 
could lag behind pressures from its rapidly growing population and 
 fast 
global 
technological advances
 . 
02 November 2026

 -

INDIA
’S COMPLEX POSITION 
IN INTERNATIONAL TRADE 
Low 
attractiveness  
due to unfavourable business environment and limited integration in global value chains  
◼ 
 Despite very strong growth, India's attractiveness is low relative to the size of its economy. The stock of foreign direct 
investment stood at only 15% of GDP in Q2 2026 compared to 52% of GDP in Vietnam and around 67% of GDP in Thailand 
and Malaysia.  
◼ 
 This lack of appeal can be attributed to 
 India’s still unfavo
 u
rable 
 business environment and 
 its 
 limited integration into 
global value chains. India’s global market share remain
 s 
 low (1.9% of total 
 world 
 exports  
 of goods
 )
, 
 especially in 
comparison with that of China (16.1% of total exports).  
◼ 
 India has not benefited from the AI boom
 , 
since its exports of AI
 -
 related goods remain modest (0.5% of its total exports)
 . 
However, 
 it has benefited from the “China +1” strategy adopted by 
 international 
 companies seeking to diversify their 
production sources. Smartphone exports by India have increased 6.3
 -
 fold over the past five years and accounted for almost 
6% of global exports in 2025 compared to less than 1% five year
 s 
earlier.  
To 
increase its a
 ttractiveness and develop its industrial sector, India has 
 multiplied F
 ree 
T
rade 
 A
greements  
◼ 
 Over the past 18 months, 
 India  
has signed a growing number of free trade agreements 
 – 
with the European Free Trade 
Association in October 2025, the United Kingdom in July 2025, Oman in December 2025, and the European Union in January 
2026
. This 
 should help to develop the industrial base and demonstrates the Modi government's commitment to 
accelerating India's integration into global trade.  
The sectors most likely to benefit from the recent trade agreement with the EU are electronics and semiconductors, 
pharmaceuticals and chemical
 s
, textile and renewable energy technologies. But expanding trade agreements without deep 
domestic improvements in the business environment, governance, education, and infrastructure will be ineffective. India 
will continue to struggle with investment reten
 tion. 
◼ 
 The US
 -
 India bilateral trade agreement has not yet been concluded. The two sides reached a framework for an interim 
Agreement in February 2026, under which the US would apply an 18% reciprocal tariff on Indian goods, with further 
reductions subject to the 
 successful conclusion of the agreement. As of early October, negotiations 
 are 
stalled. Key sticking 
points include market access, particularly 
 in 
India’s sensitive agricultural sector.

COUNTRY RISK PROFILE

Non transfer risk   
India’s external accounts are solid with a low external debt, moderate current account deficit and large FX 
reserves. 
 India’s net debtor position reached only 5.9% of GDP in Q2 2026. In the short term, external accounts 
are expected to deteriorate 
 given 
the rise in energy prices while downward pressures on the rupee should remain 
high.  
◼ 
 External accounts have remained solid 
 despite recent adverse shocks
 . 
 In 2025, external accounts remained solid 
despite the significant increase in US tariffs. In 
 H1 
 2026, they held up well against rising energy prices. Although the 
current account deficit is expected to widen for the current fiscal year as a whole, it should remain contained, and foreign 
exchange reserves should be more than sufficient to cover the co
 untry’s short
 -
 term financing needs. The 
 major  
causes 
for concern are the 
 structurally 
 low level of foreign direct investment (although it is rising) and the increased reliance on 
portfolio investment, which exposes the currency to greater volatility.  
◼ 
 Structural vulnerability to energy price shock
 . 
India is structurally vulnerable to an energy price shock as its 
 oil and gas  
trade deficit is relatively high (2.8% of GDP in 2025). However, in H1 2026, the current account withstood fairly well the 
rise in energy prices especially thanks to the sharp increase in workers’ remittances  
(+0.7 pp to 3.7% of GDP)
 . The trade 
deficit widened significantly but principally because of the strong increase in machinery and equipment imports rather

### Page 4

EcoInsight

economic
 -
 research.bnpparibas.com

4

than to the rise in the oil bill. The 
 energy 
 trade deficit increased moderately as the rise in energy prices was offset by the 
decline in imported volumes.  
However, the trade deficit is expected to widen further during the rest of the year with the significant rise in oil prices, 
and 
 the current account deficit 
 is projected  
to 
increase to 
 1.
6
% of GDP for FY 2026/2027, compared to 0.7% of GDP for FY 
2025/2026. 
 This is 
 still modest and slightly lower than during the previous energy shock  
in 2022
 .  
◼ 
 Low net FDIs.  
Although rising in H1 2026 (+0.2 point of GDP), net FDI inflows remained low at only 0.5% of GDP  
and did 
do not cover the entire current account deficit. In fact, t
 he country’s reliance on portfolio investment  
inflow
 s to meet its 
external financing needs has gradually increased over the past five years.  
◼ 
 FX reserves 
 remain very comfortable  
and represent a solid buffer protecting against external shocks
 . 
Officially, 
 they 
reached  
USD591 bn at end
 -
 October, covering 6.5 months of imports. 
 However, a
 djusted for the net short
 -
 term forward 
position (
 –
USD
200 bn, ~50% from RBI’s dollar swaps for non
 -
 resident deposits), "usable" reserves cover
 ed 
only 4.6 months 
of imports, a historic low. 
 Nonetheless, t
 his constraint is future
 -
 oriented, as much of the non
 -
 resident deposit dollars are 
due for repayment in 3
 –
5 years.  
Additionally, 
 FX 
 reserves still exceed external debt service (
 estimated at 
 USD 63 bn by 
the central bank).  
◼ 
 External debt is low (20.8% of GDP and 87.1% of total exports in Q2 2026). 
 Banks accounted for the largest share of 
external debt (30.9% of total), 
 followed by 
 non
-
 financial corporations (29.8% of total). The general government
 ’s 
 share 
reached 22.3%.   
◼ 
 Currency risk
 : 
 India is vulnerable to capital outflows and is very exposed to change in US monetary policy. 
 Its 
performance will continue to depend on energy prices and the spread between India’s and the US government bond yields.

Sovereign risk  
Public finances are structurally fragile 
 and fiscal policy room is narrow, 
 as India has one of the lowest tax bases 
in Asia and one of the highest debt interest burdens (second only to Pakistan). Nonetheless, the debt refinancing 
risk is limited given 
 its 
sound 
 profile 
 (
debt 
is 
denominated in domestic currency and held by domestic investors 
with a long maturity).

◼ 
 In FY2026 (ended in March 2026), public finances continued to improve. 
 The central government’s fiscal deficit 
narrowed to 4.4% of GDP from 5% of GDP the previous year. 
 However, t
 his reduction stemmed primarily from a significant 
rise in dividends from the central bank. 
 G
overnment revenues remained low at  
9.6% of GDP, with tax revenues stagnant 
at 
7.6% of GDP. Besides, debt
 -
 servicing costs climbed to a peak of  
3.6% of GDP, equivalent to  
37.6% of total government 
revenue
s
. 
The fiscal position is stronger than during the 2022 energy crisis, with the general government deficit estimated 
at 
7.8% of GDP  
in FY2026, compared to  
9.5% 
in FY2021/2022.  
◼ 
 For FY2027, the initial budget targets a slight reduction in the central 
 government 
 deficit, to 4.3% of GDP. This goal may 
however 
 prove challenging due to rising subsidies amid the energy shock.  
To cushion households from the shock, the government initially 
 kept 
petrol prices 
 unchanged
 . Refining firms absorbed the 
financial burden, partially offset by lower excise duties on petroleum products. Only after regional elections in mid
 -
 May 
did the government allow a modest increase in pump prices to reflect higher international 
 prices
 . 
The government has 
also increased subsidies  
to offset the impact of rising fertiliser prices on farmers.  
Revenue losses  
—
driven by  
lower excise 
duties, reduced dividends from state
 -
 owned enterprises, and the economic slowdown
 — 
could reach  
0.5% of GDP
 , while 
the cost of 
 fertiliser subsidies 
 could 
 rise from  
0.4% to 0.8% of GDP.  
To reduce the risk of fiscal slippage, the government 
has: i) raised taxes on fuel exports, ii) capped household diesel purchases; and iii) planned cuts to certain expenditures.  
◼ 
 While the primary deficit reduction  
 target may prove difficult to achieve, 
 the surge in government bond yields  
 is 
another source of severe concern
 . 
Over the first ten months of 2026
 , 
10
-
 year bond yields rose by 80 basis points, 
 and 
further increase  
is 
expected due to 
 the central bank’s 
 monetary tightening. A  
100
-
 bp 
rise in interest rates  
could push debt  
servicing costs above  
40% of 
fiscal 
 revenue by 2029
 . This is expected to 
 increase the debt
 -
 to
-
 GDP ratio 
 gradually
 .

Strong pressures on the rupee and government bond yields

◼ 
 Despite the high public debt level (84.6% of GDP in FY2026), refinancing risks remain moderate. 
 The debt structure is 
relatively safe, and the government benefits from access to 
 large 
 domestic savings. In Q2 2026, 87.8% of central 
government debt was domestic
 , and 
 70.5% was market debt
 . 
It is 
 almost entirely held by domestic financial institutions 
and denominated in local currency. 
 R
efinancing risks are limited as most debt is long term (the average maturity of central 
government debt is 13.8 years). T
 -
 bills (with a maturity of less than one year) account for only 4.2% of central government 
debt. Foreign investors held only 3.1% of 
 Treasury 
 securities in Q2 2026. 
 Finally,  
borrowings 
 in foreign currency 
 accounted 
for only 3.0% of total general government debt in FY2025/2026 (
 i.e. 
2.6% of GDP).

Systemic credit risk  
The banking sector has strengthened over the past five years
 , 
as illustrated by the improvement in 
 asset  
quality 
and capital buffers. The main sources of credit risks are rising household debt, the rise in energy prices, the 
unfavourable monsoon and monetary tightening.  
◼ 
 The quality of assets has improved significantly. 
 In Q1 2026, the banking sector’s non
 -
 performing loan (NPL) ratio fell

The Capital Adequacy Ratio (CAR) rose to 17.7% in Q1 2026, up from 17.4% a year earlier. From April 2027, Indian banks

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
OCTOBER CPI PREVIEW
Inflationary pressure fades as we move through year-end
17 November 2026
Key points
• October CPI to rise 0.41% with the annual 
pace lifting to 4.8%yr. The seasonally 
adjusted CPI lifts 0.7%mth, a small step 
up from the 0.6% increase in September.  
• October Trimmed Mean (TM) is set 
to gain 0.42%mth lifting the annual 
pace to 3.7%yr from 3.6%yr. 
• There is a small downside revision 
from our October 19th estimate for 
the December quarter CPI to 1.2%qtr 
while the TM remains 0.9%qtr.  
• The December quarter will represent the 
peak in Australian inflationary pressure. 
Our March quarter CPI remains 0.4%qtr 
and the TM is unchanged at 0.7%qtr. 
• We now see TM inflation dipping below 
3% by Q3 2027 and be well within the 
RBA’s target band by end 2027 at 2.7%yr.
• Demand remains the larger driver of 
inflation, but the recent pick-up in 
trimmed mean inflation appears to 
have been largely supply-driven.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Inflation accelerated in September...
The CPI rose 0.8% in the month of September, stronger than 
Westpac’s 0.6% estimate and the market’s expectation of 
0.7%. The annual pace picked up from 4.0%yr to 4.3%yr. The 
seasonally adjusted CPI rose 0.6% in September highlighting 
some positive seasonality in the month. 
Key factors driving the headline monthly result were: auto 
fuels (9.4%mth); domestic holiday travel & accommodation 
(8.0%mth); international holiday travel & accommodation 
(7.2%mth); and property rates & charges (5.5%mth). There 
were modest offsets from: women’s garments (–3.1%mth); 
household textiles (–6.6%mth); and accessories (–4.1%mth).
The more important underlying measure, the Trimmed Mean 
(TM), gained 0.3% in September, spot on both Westpac and 
market expectations with the annual pace lifting from 3.5%yr 
(revised from 3.6%yr) to 3.6%yr. The six-month annualised 
pace is now 4.0%yr the fastest in the short history of this 
series.
Overall, the breadth of inflation moderated for a second 
consecutive month. The share of the basket running above 
3%yr fell from 40% to 35%, while the share below 2%yr 
rose from 27% to 32%. Meanwhile, our replication of the San 
Francisco Fed’s inflation shock momentum index moved into 
positive territory after trending higher since April. Around 
14% of the CPI basket, on an expenditure-weighted basis, 
experienced positive inflation shocks in September, driven 
by holiday travel, fuel, and clothing & footwear. This was 
partly offset by negative shocks across 7% of the basket, 
concentrated in food & non-alcoholic beverages. For full 
analysis of the September CPI, see “ Inflation accelerating in 
the run up to Christmas”.
... but is set to peak in October.
Looking ahead to October, early indicators were mixed. The 
NAB business survey suggested firms continued to absorb 
a meaningful share of higher input costs, with purchase cost 
growth edging up to 2.4%qtr (+0.1ppt from September), while 
retail price growth slowed sharply to 0.2%qtr (–0.8ppts), 
partly reflecting some moderation in labour cost growth 
(see here). Meanwhile, consumers’ year-ahead inflation 
expectations rose to 5.3% in October, with this rate holding 
steady in November. Fuel prices provided a more pronounced 
source of inflationary pressure, with retail petrol and diesel 
prices jumping 8.7% and 5.3%, respectively, in October to 
average $2.40/l and $2.82/l as global oil prices approached a 
local peak.
October to mark the peak in inflation
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Neha Sharma, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
We expect the October CPI to rise 0.4% (0.41% to 2dp), lifting
annual inflation to 4.8%, which we expect to mark the peak.
On a seasonally adjusted basis, we estimate a stronger 0.7%
increase (0.68% to 2dp). For the monthly trimmed mean, we

expect a 0.4% rise (0.42% to 2dp), nudging the annual pace up
to 3.7%. On a 3m/3m basis, the TM is tracking a 1.0% increase
in October. While conceptually different from the quarterly
trimmed mean, this measure nonetheless provides a useful
guide to near-term underlying inflation momentum. For market
services excluding volatiles & holiday travel, we have pencilled
in a 0.3% rise, lifting annual inflation to 4.0% from 3.8%,
which had marked a ten-month low.

Transport is expected to make the largest contribution
(+0.35ppts), almost entirely reflecting auto fuel (+0.32ppts).
Smaller contributions are expected from clothing & footwear
(+0.06ppts), household goods & services (+0.05ppts), and
meals out & takeaway (+0.04ppts). The main offset comes
from recreation & culture (–0.18ppts), driven entirely by
holiday travel (–0.18ppts), while fruit & vegetables, utilities
and several other components provide only marginal offsets.

Key details for the month of October
Food & non-alcoholic beverages are expected to rise 0.2% in
October, lifting annual inflation to 3.3%. Strength in the month
is concentrated in meals out & takeaway, where we expect
prices to rise 0.6%mth and 4.3%yr. We have allowed for a
greater-than-usual degree of price resetting as businesses
adjust to the card surcharging ban.
Housing inflation is expected to rise 0.1% in October, with the
annual pace increasing to 6.2% reflecting base effects from

electricity. Across the major components:
• Rents are expected to rise 0.2%mth and 3.5%yr, with the
late-September indexation of the Commonwealth Rent
Assistance flowing through more broadly in October. Our
seasonally adjusted estimate is stronger at 0.4%mth.
• New dwelling inflation has been a key source of downside
surprise in recent months. We expect a 0.2%mth rise
in October, which would see the annual pace moderate
to 4.5%. The downturn in established home prices has

deepened into year-end (see here), which should weigh on
new dwelling inflation over the months ahead. Our current
estimates point to a trough of around 1.3%yr by late-2027.
• Maintenance & repair costs are expected rise a solid
0.9%mth, taking annual inflation to 3.5%, while gas & other
household fuels provide the main offset, falling 2.5%mth.
Transport is expected to be the main source of upward
pressure in October, rising 2.9%mth and lifting to 11.6%yr.

This largely reflects an 8.2%mth jump in auto fuel prices
following the rise in global oil prices through the month. Motor
vehicle prices and spare parts are also expected to increase, by
0.4%mth and 1.6%mth respectively.

Recreation & culture is expected to provide the key offset, 
falling 1.5%mth, driven by a 3.0%mth decline in holiday travel. 
We also expect some pull-back across components exposed 
to the AI trade, including audio, visual & computing equipment 
(–0.2%mth) and games, toys & hobbies (–0.3%mth).
Underlying inflation holds momentum
As noted above, our October TM estimate is also 0.4%mth, a step 
up from 0.3% in September taking the annual pace to 3.7%yr from 
3.6%yr and lifting the six month annualised pace to 4.2%yr, from 
4.0%yr, the fastest pace in the short history of the series. Rents 
and dwellings continue to underpin the TM estimates. 
Upside risks to October baseline ...
We view the risks around the October CPI outcome as slightly 
tilted to the upside. Our estimates already allow for a greater 
degree of price resetting in areas such as meals out & takeaway 
and personal and household services, but there is a risk that this 
adjustment proves broader than we have assumed. Increases in 
fuel levy surcharges in early October are another source of upside 
risk, including increases by Woolworths and Australia Post, with 
the latter nearly doubling its surcharge between September and 
October. The key downside risk remains housing, particularly 
new dwelling prices, which have surprised to the downside in 
recent months and could continue to do so as the housing market 
downturn deepens. Trade notifications data also point to some 
further downside risk, with builders on average announcing 
smaller price changes than in prior months.
... but momentum to ease in November ...
We have a preliminary estimate for the November CPI of 
–0.1%mth which will see the annual pace ease back to 4.6%yr. 
Auto fuel moved lower through November to be down –2.1% in 
the month with seasonal falls in garments, health, furniture & 
household appliances and holiday travel offsetting some strength 
in food, housing, communication and financial services. 
Seasonally adjusted the CPI is estimated to lift 0.1% in November, 
highlighting the seasonal weakness in the month, while our TM 
estimate is for a 0.2% lift seeing the annual pace ease a touch to 
3.6%yr while the six month annualised pace drops from 4.2%yr 
to 3.9%yr. This moderation is expected to continue into December 
with a preliminary TM estimate of 0.2%mth/3.6%yr. 
The following page has a table with monthly profiles for the CPI, 
seasonally adjusted CPI and the TM out to July 2027. By July 
2027 we now expect an annual pace of 3.3%yr for the CPI and 
seasonally adjusted CPI while our estimate for the TM is 3.0%yr. 
More significantly, the six month annualised pace of the TM is 
estimated to be down to 2.7%yr by June 2027, well within the 
RBA’s target band. We have also included tables breaking down 
the monthly components of the CPI through the December and 
March quarters. 
... with December quarter now the peak ...
Our December quarter CPI estimate has been revised down 
slightly to 1.2%qtr, a modest 0.05ppt revision, with the annual 
pace lifting from 3.9%yr to 4.6%yr. The seasonally adjusted

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
CPI gains 1.4% in the December quarter highlighting normally
December is a seasonally soft quarter. Our TM estimate for
Q3 has a small downward revision of 0.05ppt to 0.85% at two
decimal place but this rounds up to our published estimate of
0.9%qtr. For now that suggests downside risk for our TM

estimate as getting the quarter to round to 0.8% is much
easier than getting it to round up to 1.0% . We also note
auto fuel prices falling faster than expected which may not
only result in downside risks to out CPI estimate but also our
TM estimate due to less pass through pressure.
... with inflation moderating through 2027 ...
Westpac’s preliminary March quarter CPI estimate is just 0.4%.
Driving most of this outcome is the 7.7% decline in autofuel

(–0.29ppt) and a 3.8% fall in holiday travel (–0.23ppt) being
offset by rising food (1.0%, 0.18ppt), housing (0.7%, 0.15ppt),
health (2.6%, 0.18ppt) and education (3.5%, 0.17ppt). The
March quarter does not have a lot in the way of net seasonally
with the seasonally adjusted estimate up 0.5%. Our underlying
TM measure of inflation is estimated to rise 0.7% in the March
quarter taking the annual pace down to 3.5% while the six
month annualised pace dips from 3.8%yr to 3.2%yr.

... as a disinflationary impulse kicks in.
We have for some time expected auto fuel prices to peak in
October, 240 cpl for petrol and 282 cpl for diesel, then to
trend lower from there. Our November estimates are currently
sitting on 237 cpl and 279 cpl respectively trending down to
186 cpl for petrol and 196 cpl for diesel by December 2027.
This represents a 12.7% fall in auto fuel through the first half
of 2027 subtracting 0.42ppt off the CPI over the same period

of time. For a detailed description on how we constructed the
auto fuels profile please see: “Persistent energy pressures
have further to run”. At this stage we have not adjusted our
Brent forecast in line with the current correction underway
(see the November Market Outlook for more details why) as
we expect this to be a volatile ride. Should the current correc-
tion continue, auto fuel will represent a larger drag on the CPI
and potentially the Trimmed Mean.

Critical to our expected moderation in core inflation is a step
down in dwelling price inflation on the back of falling estab-
lished home prices. From a 1.0%qtr increase in Q3 2026 we
see dwelling price increasing by 0.4% in Q4 2026, and then
0.5% in Q1 and 0.4%qtr in Q2 before dropping to 0.2%qtr in
Q3 and Q4. This will see dwelling price inflation in the CPI
drop from 5.4%yr at June 2026 to 2.3%yr by June 2027 then

down to 1.3%yr by December 2027.
At least to the end of this year there is still a high degree of
uncertainty about how much businesses will be able to pass
through upstream cost pressures. The latest NAB business sur-
vey continues to highlight robust input price pressure but also

that firms are struggling to pass on those increases.
For businesses, it is almost a now or never opportunity to raise
prices. We expect that through 2027, as energy prices ease
and the domestic economy slows under the weight of higher
interest rates and falling established home prices, households
will be much more resistant to price increases especially if dis-
cretionary household spending softens by more than expected.
That said, these headwinds may be at least partially offset by

the stronger starting point for household incomes, which has
proven more resilient than previously anticipated, and a possi-
ble bounce back in energy prices due to renewed conflict in the
Middle East.
Supply or demand driven inflation?
Inflation has steadily drifted away from the RBA’s target over
recent quarters. While the demand side is the larger driver of
inflation currently, most of the recent pick-up in TM inflation
appears to be from the supply side, and the more detailed

breakdown may understate how much.
There are several ways to split inflation into supply and de-
mand. One is to estimate how much of the rise a standard de-
mand-driven model can explain and attribute the rest to supply
(see here). Run through to Q3 2026, this approach puts most of
the recent pick-up in TM inflation down to supply. Without it,
trimmed mean would have risen from around 3.0%yr to 3.2%yr
over the year to Q3 2026, rather than the 2.9%yr to 3.7%yr

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
rise we actually saw. The supply contribution is moderate,
though, and well below its Covid-era peak. This analysis also
reveals that most of the disinflation through late-2023 and into
early-2025 reflected supply pressures unwinding, while de-
mand-driven inflation remained stickier, persisting above 3%yr.

Another is the San Francisco Fed approach, which the RBA has
also replicated (see here). The attribution is that if prices rise
and quantities fall, it is supply; if both rise, it is demand. We run
it to Q2 2026, as Q3 volume data are not yet available. Like the
first approach, it finds that demand accounts for most of infla-
tion (headline in this case). However, unlike the first approach,
it shows supply contributions easing a little.
A closer look at the categories classified as demand tells a

more nuanced story. Utilities, health and insurance all feature,
and their prices are largely shaped by government policy or are
not the categories that usually come to mind when people talk
about demand-driven inflation, and they are not very sensitive
to interest rates. You can look at electricity as a clear example
where prices in recent history have been set by government
policies (the introduction of, and then removal of, cost of living
rebates as well as policies to decarbonise the economy) or ex-

ternal supply shock (rising gas prices pushing up the marginal
cost of generation).
Categories more commonly thought of discretionary do show
up on the demand side, with recreation & culture (excluding
travel) and meals out & takeaway being the key ones. The
drawback of this sort of decomposition is that it labels cate-
gories where prices and quantities move together as demand,
even when this may reflect the ripple effects of a supply shock
elsewhere, a point our Chief Economist Luci Ellis detailed in a

note in 2024 (see In a world gone bananas, don’t squash the
other fruit). Some of this may be occurring now. In particular,
the analysis is likely not capturing the shift in spending towards
these categories resulting from travel disruptions associated
with the Middle East conflict (travel services itself was classi-
fied as a supply-side contributor in Q2). As travel normalises,
some of the “demand” contribution from recreation & culture
and meals out & takeaway should therefore fade over coming

quarters.
We would also acknowledge the work of Tim Toohey and his
analysis “The RBA has an identity problem”.

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
It has been an action-packed few weeks for markets. The US
2026 mid-term elections saw a repudiation of President Trump
with the Democrats now set to take control over both houses
of Congress. The outcome and easing Middle East tensions

saw Brent crude oil fall from just under US$120/bbl to roughly
US$95/bbl, back around the post-Iran-conflict average. The
associated easing in inflation fears has supported a continued
descent in bond yields and rally in risk sentiment – albeit with
significant ‘wrinkles’ around a high US inflation read and an
AI-led pull-back in equity markets that has been compounded
by the sudden resignation of NVIDIA’s CEO.
Many central banks have already begun re-tightening policy

– with the Bank of Canada and Bank of England the latest
additions to the pack – but what comes next will differ widely
across jurisdictions. In the US, the combination of strong
activity and sticky inflation have led FOMC members to more
strongly advocate for further tightening. We have added a
January hike to the December move already in our forecasts,
before a long pause and eventual cuts in 2028.
Europe shares has similar inflation concerns but not so much

strength on the growth front. The ECB sees activity near
trend and is uncomfortable with inflation at 3.8%yr, so we
expect a December hike as insurance. Unlike market pricing
though, we think this will be the last, with energy and
investment-related price pressures easing through 2027 and
scope to ease back towards neutral in 2028. China faces a
very different challenge – not too much demand but too little.
Industrial strength and technology investment are providing

support but we expect policy direction and stimulus will be
needed to turn household demand, with this expected to
come in 2027 and property the most likely catalyst.
The RBA meanwhile followed its September hike with another
in November, but the Governor’s call to ‘take stock’ signals a
December pause and we think the hiking phase is now more
clearly over. We continue to pencil in a move to cuts in the
second half of 2027. While a February hike is not completely

out of the question, policy is clearly restrictive and downside
risks are crystallising. Reflecting this, we have downgraded
our GDP, labour market and housing market forecasts for 2027.
Australia: The housing downturn has been sharper than
expected and is set to spill over more materially to the broader
economy. The scale of these spillovers should become clearer
over coming months, keeping the RBA on hold as downside
risks to growth build. Business investment remains a source

of resilience, led by data centres and renewables, but rising
costs and growing community opposition have seen some
projects shelved. Still, the supply-side picture is constructive,
with strong investment in the market sector driving capital
deepening and productivity gains, albeit uneven ones.
Commodities: The commodities complex was a story of two
halves in October, with sharp gains across energy offsetting
weakness elsewhere and lifting our broadest commodities

index 0.6%mth. Reflecting continued energy-market tightness,
we expect Brent to average US$101/bbl in Q4 and have raised
our Q4 Japanese LNG forecast to US$27.
Global FX Markets: Of late, risks have favoured the US
dollar and FOMC member concerns have been decidedly one-
sided towards higher inflation. The DXY has consequently
continued to test the upper limit of its 18-month range, but
remains inside it.
New Zealand: A busy month has included a tight – and

currently inconclusive – general election, a surprise hold by the
RBNZ in October and a somewhat disappointing Q3 labour
market report. However, the medium-term outlook remains little
changed, with a recovering economy and above-target inflation
likely to lead to further monetary tightening in coming months.
Pacific: Distance from major markets, small domestic
economies and a heavy reliance on imported fuel and food
mean global disruptions tend to reach the Pacific at a higher

cost. However, most Pacific countries face the latest shock
with somewhat better buffers.
United States: Data suggests the US carried strong
momentum in to H2 2026. But questions are being asked over
the profitability of AI deployment, household real incomes are
trending flat to down, and term interest rates continue to weigh.
China: China GDP surprised to the upside in Q3, keeping
authorities on track for 2026, but more importantly offering a
little more time to accelerate domestic demand in to 2027.

Asia: We expect the BoJ to raise its policy rate by 25bps to
1.50% in December, before gradually taking it to 2.0% through
2027. The case for further tightening rests on the evidence that
firms are more willing to raise prices, labour scarcity supports
persistent wage growth, which should in turn support spending.
Europe & UK: The ECB left its key policy rates unchanged in
October. Another hike is forecast in December. Against market
pricing, we expect this will prove the last for the current cycle.

Taking stock

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
All major central banks are now in a rate-hiking phase. The
stance of policy nonetheless varies considerably across
economies, as does the policy outlook.
The Bank of England and Bank of Canada increased their
policy rates for the first time this cycle and the RBA a fifth time

since the last Market Outlook. The pace of increase is generally
moderate: the FOMC and RBNZ paused at their latest
meetings – the RBNZ unexpectedly so – but both are expected
to lift policy rates further. Policy is likely still accommodative
in New Zealand and Canada, consistent with the economic and
labour market slack evident in these economies. In Australia it
is clearly restrictive, as discussed below.
The US is contending with sticky inflation, as evidenced by

recent CPI and labour market data. We have long been of the
view that the US is likely to be structurally more inflationary
than many peers, and concerns about inflation are increasingly
coming to the fore in FOMC members’ comments. Accordingly
we have added another hike in January to our US rates view, on
top of the one in December already forecast (see page 20).
The main, but not sole, driver of the pivot to tightening has been
the energy price shock. That said, exclusion-based core inflation

measures (excluding food and energy) are also above central
bank targets. Market pricing of future central bank moves had
been sensitive to oil prices and the outlook in the Middle East
earlier in the year. That correlation broke down during the bond
sell-off, which was mostly a phenomenon of real yields, but
seems to have  re-emerged in the pricing for policy in 2027.
Spot and daated Brent oil prices have fallen markedly in recent
days as tensions have eased and negotiations appear to have

restarted. This has not affected very near-term pricing of central
bank decisions, and an initial moderation of mid-2027 pricing
retraced after the strong US CPI print and some Fed speeches.
Further along the yield curve, last month’s sell-off in bond
markets has largely reversed. Unlike the increase in yields,
when longer-run market-based measures of inflation
expectations relatively stable, the more recent rally has
involved some decline in these expectations measures. Most of

the adjustment has been front-loaded: the 1-year expectations
derived from swaps have declined 45bps for Australia and
nearly 60bps for the US since the beginning of November.
However, inflation expectations derived for future years have
also moderated. The 5-year-5-year forward measures derived
from swaps and breakevens have both declined, with the latter
noticeably below the 2.5% RBA target midpoint. The same
measures for the US have also declined over this period, but by

less, and remain further above the Fed’s 2% target, consistent
with our view about inflation pressures there.
Global asset market rotation ...
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
... while the RBA nears a pause
The rally in long bond markets has occurred at the same time as
equity markets have tended to sell off, particularly in the tech
sector. Another wave of scepticism around the sustainability
of the data centre build-out has formed and found expression

in the equity prices of many semiconductor manufacturers and
other related firms. One possible trigger for the latest sell-off
may have been the release of Anthropic’s prospectus ahead
of its IPO. As well as the more existential risks flagged in the
documents, its stated plans for further data centre investment
have concentrated focus on the scale of the data centre and AI
boom more broadly, and the enormous funding task involved.
The RBA Monetary Policy Board (MPB) hiked at its November

meeting following up the late September hike as expected. As
we noted at the time, the MPB was responding to the apparent
crystallisation of some of the risks that it had flagged back in
August. In particular, the re-emergence of pressures on fuel
prices led to fears of a further wave of pass-through of energy
costs into the prices of other goods and services. The Q3
outcome for underlying inflation outstripped expectations and
Q4 is also likely to be uncomfortably high. With inflation already

materially above target, the RBA is worried that inflation
expectations could be rising, pointing to liaison and business
survey evidence. So far, though, other business surveys suggest
that pass-through has been less severe than the original wave
of price increases at the outbreak of the Middle East conflict.
In the post-meeting media conference, the Governor flagged
that it was a good time to “take stock” of the policy tightening
to date. Both we and the market read this as a signal that the

MPB would pause at the December meeting. The 6:3 split vote
at the meeting also suggests that some external members are
more attentive to downside risks than the internal members.
We think the most likely outcome from here is that the RBA
has ended its rate-hiking phase, and that a cutting phase will
begin in the second half of 2027. There is still a risk of a hike
in February, and we believe the MPB will at least discuss the
option at that meeting. The staff and senior officials remain

sensitive to inflation risks, and have a downbeat view of
supply capacity growth. The latter meant that the RBA had to
upgrade its forecasts for the labour force participation rate in
its However, policy is clearly restrictive, and while there are
upside risks to inflation, there are clearly downside risks, too.
With markets now expecting that the RBA is likely at the end
of its hiking phase, while most peers are expected to have 2–3
hikes to go, short-end yield differentials have narrowed. This

has been reflected in the AUD exchange rate, which has tended
to underperform in recent days. We have tempered our near-
term outlook for the AUD, but note that the TWI remains abourt
4% above the 2022–2024 average, and that this appreciation
will be contributing to the disinflationary impact of tight policy.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
The housing downturn has been sharper than expected and
is set to spill over more materially to the broader economy.
The scale of these spillovers should become clearer over
coming months, keeping the RBA on hold as downside risks

to growth build. Business investment remains a source of
resilience, led by data centres and renewables, but signs
of strain are emerging, with rising costs and growing
community opposition seeing some projects shelved. The
2026 Annual National Accounts offered a more constructive
supply-side picture, with strong investment across parts of
the market sector driving capital deepening and supporting
productivity, but this has been uneven.
As set out on page 26, we expect the lift in inflation this year

to subside through 2027, with underlying measures easing
back from 3.7%yr currently to 2.7%yr by end 2027 and 2.3%yr
in 2028. Indeed, the deeper housing downturn and associated
spillovers give us greater conviction in this view. The
correction is now the largest nominal price decline on records
going back to 1980 and appears to have accelerated following
the November 25bp RBA rate hike. This is on the back of
dismal dwelling finance approvals outcome which showed

the value of new loans plunged 15.5%qtr in Q3, the biggest
quarterly decline on records going back to 1991.
This downturn has some clear direct effects. The housing
sector, covering both real estate transactions and residential
construction, accounts for around 5–6% of GDP. The drop
in turnover will take about 0.4ppts off GDP growth over
calendar 2026 (captured through the “ownership transfer
costs” component of expenditure GDP) with the downturn also

expected to have a significant drag on dwelling construction
heading into 2027.
Steepest housing downturn in almost 50 years ...
Pat Bustamante, Senior Economist
However, the impact goes well beyond these direct effects.
Falling housing wealth will weigh on household spending,
while the residential building has strong linkages to the rest of
the economy. Residential investment supports activity across
manufacturing, transport and professional services; dollar

for dollar, we estimate its broader economic impact is around
1.5 times that of consumer spending. The flow on impacts via
employment and broader confidence are also substantive.
We have lowered our forecasts for household consumption,
ownership transfer costs and dwelling investment.
Consumption growth is now expected to trough at 1.1%yr
during 2027, from 1.8%yr forecast in our October Market
Outlook, before recovering to 2.7%yr in 2028. Ownership

transfer costs are expected to see a 25%yr decline over 2026,
the sharpest decline since the March quarter 1990, even
larger than what we saw during the pandemic. New dwelling
investment is expected to contract by 1.0%yr in 2027, rather
than grow by 2.1%yr as we expected back in October, before
recovering by 3.2%yr in 2028, down from 4.9%yr. Together
with some flow-on dampening effect on employment, these
revisions lower our GDP growth forecast to just 1.0%yr in

2027, from 1.6%yr, and 2.1%yr in 2028, from 2.5%yr.
We do not expect the slowdown to become immediately
apparent in the data. Consumer spending looks to have been
relatively resilient through the September quarter. Discretionary
services spend has been notably firm in particular, with only
some of this due to a catch-up on travel and tourism spend
disrupted by the Middle East conflict earlier in the year.
Household disposable incomes were still relatively well

supported through the quarter with some lift coming from tax
cuts and the FWC award wage increase. However, our card
tracker data suggests discretionary spending momentum has
faded noticeable over the first half of the December quarter.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
... to drive a sharp economic slowdown ...
The confidence impacts from housing downturn may also be
starting to show. While the drop in consumer sentiment back
to extreme lows has more to do with rate rises and fuel prices,
the weakening in business confidence became more prominent

in October with a notably sharp fall in forward orders. While
this has not had a flow-on impact on employment and capex
expectations to date, these will likely soften if confidence
remains negative and the signal from forward order starts to
be reflected in wider business conditions.
Business investment is still likely to be an important offset, with
data centres and renewables supporting activity. But even here,
some cracks are starting to emerge. Reports of project delays

in NSW and South Australia amid community opposition pose
limited risk to projects already underway but could increasingly
affect the pipeline of proposed data-centre investment (with
the notably cancellation of an IPO for a local operator as well).
Sentiment across the AI sector more broadly has also weakened
with equity valuations dropping, leadership instability at NVIDIA.
Anthropic’s ‘mega IPO’ due November 18 and expected to land
at market valuation of ~US$1trn will be an important test of how

fundamental the shift in sentiment may have been.
Annual National Accounts
The Annual National Accounts (the ‘Annuals’) provide updated
benchmarks and some useful detail including on investment
trends across industries, the capital stock and productivity.
The 2025–26 figures show somewhat firmer economic
activity and business investment than previously estimated,
particularly in 2024–25. The economy grew by 2.3% in
year-average terms, broadly in line with the initial quarterly

National Accounts estimate of 2.4%. However, growth in
2024–25 was revised up by 0.3ppts to 1.6%, largely reflecting
stronger private business investment. Business investment
is now estimated to have increased by 1.1% in 2024–25,
compared with the initial estimate of a 0.2% decline. The
revisions do not materially alter the broader productivity story,
but they do suggest that the economy was growing a little
more strongly but with the same degree of inflation in 2025.

The Annuals also provide a richer picture of the supply side,
including investment by industry and the evolution of the capital
stock, details that are not available in the quarterly accounts.
These data point to a notable lift in investment in the market (ex-
mining) part of the economy, where investment has gone from
around 10% of nominal GDP in to just over 11.5%. Outside of
this, investment has been softer, particularly in the mining sector.
Within the market sector, investment by the IT industry,

increasingly focused on data centres, has risen to its highest
share of nominal GDP since the technology boom of the
1990s. Utilities investment, including renewables and
associated infrastructure, is stronger still, at its highest share
since 1983, while investment by the professional services
industry has reached a record high. Across the non-market
sector (including health, education and public administration),
investment has also held at a slightly higher share of the

economy, driven by the health and public administration
(including defence) industries.
The investment upswing is also beginning to translate into
capital deepening as industries look to rebuild capital stocks
after a period of soft growth. Outside mining, market-sector
capital per hour worked is at a record high and well above its pre-
pandemic level. That is an encouraging development, particularly
given that the sector accounts for 70% of total employment.

There are important divergences beneath the aggregate. Mining
continues to experience capital shallowing, as investment has
been insufficient to cover wear and tear and capital requirements
associated with additional workers. By contrast, the capital stock
in the non-market sector appears to have caught up with the
surge in employment we have seen since the pandemic, with
capital per worker increasing for the first time since the pandemic.

### Page 8

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
Capital deepening will help support productivity growth
across the economy. The Annuals show that productivity in the
market sector (excluding mining) continues to grow by around
1% in year-average terms, while mining remains a drag on

aggregate productivity (down 0.8% in 2025–26 and almost
30% since 2017).
At the same time, a major recent headwind is easing as the rapid
expansion of the non-market sector slows. This matters for
aggregate productivity. Productivity growth reflects both gains
within industries, through capital deepening, technology and
better processes, and the ‘reallocation’ of resources between
industries with different productivity levels.
Measured productivity in the non-market sector is considerably

lower than across much of the market sector, partly reflecting
measurement issues (output is generally not well measured,
particularly when there are quality changes). As employment
shifted towards these industries, this compositional effect
weighed on aggregate productivity. Our estimates suggest it
has reduced the level of aggregate productivity by a significant
1ppt over the past 15 years and almost 0.3ppts over the three
years to 2025–26.
What drove growth in market-sector productivity?

It wasn’t only capital deepening that drove productivity growth.
Multifactor productivity (MFP), which captures how efficiently
businesses combine labour and capital through changes in
processes, technology, know-how, was the dominant driver of
market-sector productivity growth this year. This gives us more
conviction that the supply side is normalising, and productivity
growth will improve from its recent lows.
At the industry level, some of those recording stronger

investment are also beginning to deliver stronger productivity
outcomes, although the picture remains uneven.
... as non-mining sector rebuilds capital, productivity
IT stands out at the positive end of the spectrum, alongside
improving outcomes in manufacturing, administrative and
support services, and transport. These are also industries
where the diffusion of AI and other digital technologies could
deliver further productivity gains. Utilities and professional

services should also benefit as stronger investment translates
into greater capital intensity and efficiency.
At the other end, mining remains a major drag, consistent
with the capital shallowing evident in the investment and
capital-stock data. Construction is another notable laggard,
with productivity continuing to deteriorate despite strong
demand from housing, population growth, renewable-
energy infrastructure and data-centre projects. Construction

productivity fell around 2% in 2025–26 and almost 21% over
the 13 years from 2012–13 to 2025–26.
Bottom line
The Annuals provide a more constructive supply-side picture
than the aggregate productivity figures alone suggest. Market-
sector productivity outside mining continues to advance, the
reallocation drag from the expanding non-market sector looks
likely to fade, and capital deepening is strengthening across
important parts of the economy. The strong MFP outcome in

the market sector gives us more conviction that conviction that
the supply side is normalising, and productivity growth will
improve going ahead.
The critical question is whether the strong investment
underway in technology, professional services, electricity and
related infrastructure translates into stronger productivity
growth as that new capital is put to work. If it does, the
improvement in capital intensity evident in the Annuals
could mark the beginning of a more durable lift in Australia’s

productivity performance.
